<a id='question-111'></a>

### Question 111: Renovascular Hypertension: One-Kidney, One-Clip Model
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Pathophysiology of volume-dependent renovascular hypertension secondary to bilateral renal artery stenosis or stenosis in a solitary kidney, characterized by initial renin activation followed by chronic suppression due to massive volume expansion. (双侧肾动脉狭窄或单肾狭窄导致容量依赖性高血压的病理生理机制，特点是最初的肾素激活，随后由于巨大的容量扩张而慢性抑制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male research subject undergoes a surgical procedure to investigate the pathophysiology of hypertension. The procedure involves the removal of his left kidney (left nephrectomy) and the placement of a constricting clip on the right renal artery. Immediately following the procedure, the subject's blood pressure increases significantly. Serial measurements over the next three weeks reveal persistently elevated blood pressure (mean arterial pressure ~140 mmHg), despite the absence of significant changes in plasma renin activity (PRA), which remains within the normal range (4-10 ng/mL/hr). Urinalysis shows no proteinuria or hematuria. Serum creatinine increases from a baseline of 0.8 mg/dL to 1.5 mg/dL. A 24-hour urine collection reveals sodium excretion of 50 mEq/day (normal < 250 mEq/day) and water excretion of 1.0 L/day (normal ~1.5-2.5 L/day). Plasma aldosterone concentration (PAC) is also within the normal range (2-15 ng/dL). Which of the following best explains the persistently elevated blood pressure despite normal plasma renin activity in this subject?

(A) The initial reduction in renal perfusion pressure caused a transient increase in renin release, which has now normalized as the kidney adapts to the chronic hypoperfusion. However, the underlying renal artery stenosis remains, causing persistent activation of the sympathetic nervous system, leading to sustained hypertension independent of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS).
(B) The reduction in glomerular filtration rate (GFR) due to the renal artery stenosis leads to decreased sodium and water excretion, causing volume expansion. This volume expansion increases venous return and cardiac output, leading to increased blood pressure. The increased blood pressure suppresses renin release via the juxtaglomerular apparatus, resulting in normal PRA despite hypertension.
(C) The surgical removal of one kidney and stenosis of the remaining renal artery causes a significant decrease in total body sodium. This sodium depletion stimulates aldosterone secretion, leading to sodium and water retention, which increases blood volume and blood pressure. The increased blood pressure then suppresses renin release, leading to normal PRA.
(D) The decreased renal perfusion pressure activates the sympathetic nervous system, leading to increased norepinephrine release. This causes vasoconstriction and increases blood pressure. The elevated blood pressure stimulates baroreceptors, which inhibit sympathetic outflow and renin release, resulting in normal PRA despite hypertension.
(E) The initial decrease in renal perfusion pressure stimulates renin release, leading to increased angiotensin II and aldosterone production. These hormones cause sodium and water retention, increasing blood volume and blood pressure. However, the chronic hypertension leads to increased natriuresis and diuresis, which eventually normalizes blood volume and suppresses renin release, resulting in normal PRA.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Procedure:** Left nephrectomy + right renal artery clip (1K1C model). This creates severe stenosis in the remaining kidney.
*   **Initial BP:** Increases significantly (expected due to reduced perfusion).
*   **Chronic BP (3 weeks):** Persistently elevated (~140 mmHg).
*   **Chronic PRA:** Normal (4-10 ng/mL/hr). This is the key finding – hypertension *without* high renin.
*   **Serum Creatinine:** Increased (0.8 -> 1.5 mg/dL). Indicates reduced GFR due to stenosis.
*   **Urine Sodium:** Decreased (50 mEq/day). Indicates impaired sodium excretion.
*   **Urine Water:** Decreased (1.0 L/day). Indicates impaired water excretion.
*   **Plasma Aldosterone:** Normal (2-15 ng/dL). This is also key – aldosterone is not elevated despite low sodium excretion.
*   **Urinalysis:** Normal (no proteinuria/hematuria). Rules out significant glomerular damage.

These clues point towards a mechanism where reduced renal perfusion initially activates renin, but chronic volume expansion suppresses renin despite ongoing hypertension. The low urine sodium and water excretion confirm impaired renal handling of salt and water, leading to volume retention. The normal aldosterone suggests the RAAS is not the primary driver of the volume retention in the chronic state.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
The 1K1C model mimics bilateral renal artery stenosis or stenosis in a solitary kidney.
1.  **Initial Phase:** Reduced renal perfusion pressure (due to stenosis) is sensed by the juxtaglomerular apparatus (JGA), stimulating renin release. Renin converts angiotensinogen to angiotensin I, which is then converted to angiotensin II (Ang II) by ACE. Ang II causes vasoconstriction (increasing BP) and stimulates aldosterone release from the adrenal cortex. Aldosterone increases sodium and water reabsorption in the distal nephron. This leads to an initial increase in blood pressure and volume.
2.  **Chronic Phase:** The critical point is the *absence* of a normal contralateral kidney. In a normal individual with unilateral stenosis, the contralateral kidney can compensate by increasing its GFR and excreting more sodium and water (pressure natriuresis), preventing significant volume expansion and limiting the rise in blood pressure. In the 1K1C model, this compensatory mechanism is absent.
3.  **Volume Expansion:** The impaired sodium and water excretion by the stenotic kidney, driven by Ang II and aldosterone (initially), leads to progressive extracellular fluid volume expansion.
4.  **Hypertension & Renin Suppression:** The expanded extracellular fluid volume increases venous return, cardiac output, and ultimately, systemic arterial pressure. This elevated blood pressure, along with the increased atrial stretch and potentially increased renal perfusion pressure (despite the stenosis, the overall systemic pressure is high), suppresses renin release from the JGA.
5.  **Normal PRA:** Consequently, despite the severe hypertension, plasma renin activity becomes normal or even low. The hypertension is now driven primarily by the expanded blood volume (volume-dependent hypertension), not by excessive RAAS activation. The normal aldosterone level further supports this, as the primary driver of sodium retention is the volume expansion itself, potentially overriding normal aldosterone regulation or reflecting a less potent effect of Ang II on aldosterone in this specific context. The reduced GFR contributes to the impaired sodium excretion, exacerbating the volume retention.

##### 3. Renal Microcirculation & Autoregulation (肾微循环与自动调节)
The stenosis itself causes reduced renal plasma flow (RPF) and GFR. The afferent arteriole constricts in response to reduced pressure and potentially Ang II, while the efferent arteriole may dilate slightly (though Ang II can constrict it). This increases intraglomerular pressure initially, but the overall GFR falls. The reduced GFR impairs the kidney's ability to excrete sodium and water, contributing to volume overload. Autoregulatory mechanisms (myogenic response, tubuloglomerular feedback) are overwhelmed by the severe stenosis and systemic hypertension.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** While sympathetic activation can contribute to renovascular hypertension, the key feature of the *chronic* 1K1C model is the *normalization* of renin despite hypertension. This option suggests persistent sympathetic activation as the primary cause of *normal* renin, which is inconsistent with the pathophysiology. The initial phase involves sympathetic activation, but it's not the explanation for the *chronic* state with normal renin.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Sodium depletion stimulates aldosterone, which causes sodium and water retention. However, the 1K1C model leads to *sodium retention* due to impaired excretion, not depletion. The primary problem is the kidney's inability to excrete sodium effectively, leading to volume expansion, not initial sodium depletion.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Sympathetic activation does increase blood pressure, but like option A, it doesn't explain the *normal* renin level in the chronic phase. Furthermore, while sympathetic activation is involved, the primary mechanism for the chronic hypertension with normal renin is volume expansion due to impaired sodium excretion.
- (E): **Incorrect.** The initial phase involves renin activation leading to increased Ang II and aldosterone, causing sodium/water retention and volume expansion. However, this option incorrectly states that chronic hypertension leads to *increased* natriuresis and diuresis, which would *lower* blood volume and suppress renin. In reality, the chronic state is characterized by *impaired* natriuresis and diuresis due to the stenotic kidney and volume expansion, leading to sustained hypertension and renin suppression.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The 1K1C model demonstrates volume-dependent renovascular hypertension. Chronic hypertension in this setting is characterized by significant volume expansion and suppressed renin activity, distinguishing it from renin-dependent hypertension (e.g., bilateral renal artery stenosis in a patient with two normal kidneys, where renin would be high). Key differentials include primary aldosteronism (high aldosterone, suppressed renin), Cushing's syndrome (various mechanisms), and essential hypertension (complex, often involving RAAS but not typically with normal renin and severe volume overload like this).

<a id='question-112'></a>

### Question 112: Fibromuscular Dysplasia (FMD) and Renovascular Hypertension
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Fibromuscular dysplasia (FMD) is a non-atherosclerotic, non-inflammatory vascular disease characterized by specific angiographic findings, particularly the "string of beads" appearance, typically affecting the middle-to-distal segments of renal arteries in young women, leading to renovascular hypertension. (纤维肌发育不良是一种非动脉粥样硬化、非炎症性血管疾病，其特征是特定的血管造影发现，特别是“念珠状”外观，通常影响年轻女性肾动脉的中远端，导致肾血管性高血压。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 26-year-old female presents to the emergency department complaining of severe headaches and neck pain for the past week. Her past medical history is unremarkable, and she denies smoking or illicit drug use. Her blood pressure is markedly elevated at 175/105 mmHg. Physical examination reveals a right cervical carotid bruit and a systolic-diastolic epigastric bruit. She reports intermittent pulsatile tinnitus in her right ear. Laboratory studies show normal serum electrolytes, BUN, and creatinine. Urinalysis is unremarkable. Renal angiography is performed due to suspicion of renovascular hypertension. The angiogram reveals alternating segments of stenosis and dilatation in the middle-to-distal portion of the right renal artery, creating a characteristic "string of beads" appearance.

Which of the following angiographic findings is most characteristic of medial fibroplasia causing renovascular hypertension in this patient?

(A) Proximal ostial stenosis with intimal thickening and calcification.
(B) Multiple, small, saccular aneurysms distributed throughout the renal vasculature.
(C) A smooth, tapered stenosis at the origin of the renal artery.
(D) A "string of beads" appearance involving the middle-to-distal segments of the renal artery.
(E) Occlusion of the renal artery with collateral vessel formation.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** Young female (26 years old) - FMD predominantly affects women aged 15-50.
- **Symptoms:** Severe refractory hypertension (175/105 mmHg), pulsatile tinnitus (right ear), neck pain, headaches - Classic symptoms associated with FMD affecting renal and carotid arteries.
- **Physical Exam:** Right cervical carotid bruit, systolic-diastolic epigastric bruit - Suggests vascular abnormalities in the carotid and renal arteries, respectively.
- **Laboratory/Urinalysis:** Normal renal function tests (BUN, Cr, electrolytes) and urinalysis - Rules out acute kidney injury or significant glomerular disease as the primary cause of hypertension.
- **Angiography:** "String of beads" appearance in the middle-to-distal right renal artery - This is the pathognomonic angiographic finding for medial fibroplasia, the most common type of FMD. The alternating stenosis and dilatation result from fibromuscular ridges and thinning of the vessel wall.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **FMD Pathophysiology:** FMD is characterized by abnormal cell growth in the artery wall, replacing elastic tissue and smooth muscle with fibrous tissue. Medial fibroplasia, the most common subtype (~85-90%), specifically affects the media layer.
- **Angiographic Appearance:** The fibromuscular ridges cause stenosis, while the intervening areas of weakened vessel wall bulge outwards, creating the characteristic alternating pattern of stenosis and dilatation seen as a "string of beads" on angiography. This pattern typically affects the middle and distal segments of the renal artery, unlike atherosclerosis which usually affects the proximal ostium.
- **Renovascular Hypertension:** The stenosis reduces blood flow to the affected kidney, activating the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS). Increased renin release leads to angiotensin II production, causing vasoconstriction and sodium/water retention, resulting in hypertension. The contralateral kidney is usually unaffected, but the overall effect is systemic hypertension.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's age, sex, symptoms (headache, tinnitus, neck pain), physical findings (bruits), and the specific angiographic pattern ("string of beads" in the middle-to-distal renal artery) are all highly consistent with FMD causing renovascular hypertension.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Proximal ostial stenosis with intimal thickening and calcification.** This description is characteristic of atherosclerosis, which typically affects older individuals and involves the proximal portion of the renal artery. Atherosclerosis involves plaque formation within the intima, leading to luminal narrowing, often with calcification. This is inconsistent with the patient's age and the described angiographic findings.
- (B): **Multiple, small, saccular aneurysms distributed throughout the renal vasculature.** This description is more suggestive of conditions like polyarteritis nodosa (PAN) or other vasculitides, which can cause multiple small aneurysms. FMD typically causes stenosis and dilatation, not widespread aneurysms.
- (C): **A smooth, tapered stenosis at the origin of the renal artery.** This description is characteristic of Takayasu arteritis, a large vessel vasculitis that typically affects young women and involves the aorta and its major branches, including the renal arteries. However, Takayasu arteritis usually causes a smooth, tapered stenosis at the origin, not the "string of beads" pattern in the middle/distal segments seen in FMD.
- (E): **Occlusion of the renal artery with collateral vessel formation.** Complete occlusion is a severe complication that can occur in FMD or atherosclerosis, but it is not the characteristic angiographic finding described in the vignette. The "string of beads" pattern implies alternating stenosis and dilatation, not complete blockage. Collateral vessels might develop over time in response to chronic stenosis, but occlusion itself is not the defining feature.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Fibromuscular dysplasia (FMD) is a key cause of renovascular hypertension in young women. The classic angiographic finding is the "string of beads" appearance, typically involving the middle-to-distal segments of the renal artery. This pattern distinguishes FMD from atherosclerosis (proximal ostial stenosis) and Takayasu arteritis (smooth, tapered stenosis at the origin). Remember FMD is non-atherosclerotic and non-inflammatory.

<a id='question-113'></a>

### Question 113: Captopril Renal Scintigraphy in Renal Artery Stenosis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Captopril renal scintigraphy mechanism in renal artery stenosis (RAS) involves ACE inhibition leading to efferent arteriole dilation and subsequent GFR drop in the affected kidney due to compromised autoregulation. (血管紧张素转化酶抑制剂曲唑巴嗪肾闪烁扫描机制：在肾动脉狭窄中，ACE抑制导致高氯仿效性肾脏的夹层动脉扩张，随后GFR下降，因为其自主调节能力受损。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents with refractory hypertension despite triple-drug therapy (ACE inhibitor, beta-blocker, calcium channel blocker). He has a history of smoking and hyperlipidemia. Physical examination reveals a grade III/VI systolic bruit over the left flank. Renal function tests show serum creatinine 1.8 mg/dL (baseline 1.2 mg/dL) and BUN 35 mg/dL. Urinalysis is unremarkable. A renal duplex ultrasound suggests possible left renal artery stenosis. To further evaluate the hemodynamic significance of the suspected stenosis, a captopril renal scintigraphy is performed using Technetium-99m mercaptoacetyltriglycine (Tc-99m MAG3). Baseline imaging shows symmetric tracer uptake in both kidneys. Following oral administration of 25 mg captopril, follow-up scintigraphy demonstrates a marked and persistent delay in tracer arrival and excretion in the left kidney compared to the right kidney, with the left kidney showing significantly reduced overall tracer activity. What is the primary physiological mechanism by which captopril induces this change in the left kidney?

(A) Captopril inhibits the reabsorption of Tc-99m MAG3 in the proximal tubule, leading to decreased excretion in the stenotic kidney due to impaired tubular function.
(B) Captopril blocks the conversion of angiotensin I to angiotensin II, causing systemic vasodilation and a decrease in renal perfusion pressure, which affects both kidneys equally but is more pronounced in the stenotic kidney due to its already compromised blood flow.
(C) Captopril directly inhibits the Na+/K+-ATPase pump in the distal tubule, impairing the secretion of potassium and hydrogen ions, leading to reduced tracer excretion.
(D) Captopril blocks angiotensin II synthesis, leading to dilation of the efferent arteriole in the stenotic kidney, which collapses intraglomerular hydrostatic pressure and acutely reduces GFR, thereby decreasing tracer filtration and excretion.
(E) Captopril stimulates the release of bradykinin, which causes afferent arteriolar constriction in the stenotic kidney, reducing GFR and tracer filtration.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male with refractory hypertension, smoking, hyperlipidemia – risk factors for atherosclerosis and renal artery stenosis (RAS).
- **Physical Exam:** Left flank bruit – suggestive of turbulent blood flow in the left renal artery, consistent with stenosis.
- **Labs:** Elevated creatinine (1.8 mg/dL from baseline 1.2 mg/dL) and BUN (35 mg/dL) – indicate worsening renal function, possibly due to RAS.
- **Imaging:** Duplex ultrasound suggests left RAS.
- **Procedure:** Captopril renal scintigraphy using Tc-99m MAG3.
- **Baseline Scan:** Symmetric uptake – suggests both kidneys are initially functioning adequately, although the stenosis might be hemodynamically insignificant at baseline.
- **Post-Captopril Scan:** Marked delay and reduced tracer activity in the *left* kidney – this is the key finding. It indicates a significant, *unilateral* effect of captopril on the left kidney's function.
- **Question:** What is the *physiological mechanism* of this unilateral effect?

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Renal Artery Stenosis (RAS) Pathophysiology:** In unilateral RAS, blood flow to the stenotic kidney is reduced. To maintain GFR, the kidney relies heavily on Angiotensin II (Ang II). Ang II causes *afferent arteriolar constriction* (maintaining glomerular hydrostatic pressure, $P_{GC}$) and *efferent arteriolar constriction* (maintaining filtration fraction and preventing excessive fluid loss). The efferent constriction is particularly crucial in maintaining GFR in the face of reduced renal perfusion pressure ($P_{BS}$).
- **Captopril Mechanism:** Captopril is an Angiotensin-Converting Enzyme (ACE) inhibitor. It blocks the conversion of Angiotensin I to Angiotensin II.
- **Effect of Captopril in RAS:** By blocking Ang II synthesis, captopril removes the primary stimulus for efferent arteriolar constriction in the stenotic kidney. The efferent arteriole dilates significantly.
- **Consequence of Efferent Dilation:** Dilation of the efferent arteriole drastically reduces the resistance to outflow from the glomerulus. This leads to a sharp decrease in intraglomerular hydrostatic pressure ($P_{GC}$). Since GFR is directly proportional to $P_{GC}$ (GFR ∝ $P_{GC}$ - $π_{GC}$ - $P_{BS}$ + $π_{BS}$), the drop in $P_{GC}$ causes a significant, acute reduction in GFR in the stenotic kidney.
- **Scintigraphy Interpretation:** Tc-99m MAG3 is filtered by the glomerulus and actively secreted by the proximal tubule. Its excretion reflects GFR and tubular function. A significant drop in GFR due to efferent arteriole dilation caused by captopril leads to markedly reduced filtration and subsequent excretion of the tracer in the affected (left) kidney, resulting in the observed delayed and diminished uptake on the post-captopril scan. The contralateral (right) kidney, which does not rely as heavily on Ang II for GFR maintenance (or has less severe stenosis), is less affected, showing normal or even enhanced relative uptake. This differential response confirms the hemodynamic significance of the stenosis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Incorrect.** Captopril does not primarily inhibit MAG3 reabsorption in the proximal tubule. While ACE inhibitors can have complex effects on tubular transport, the dramatic change seen in the captopril scan is due to GFR reduction, not altered tubular reabsorption of the tracer. The tracer is filtered and secreted, and its excretion is primarily GFR-dependent in this context.
- (B) **Incorrect.** While captopril does cause systemic vasodilation and a decrease in systemic blood pressure, which could affect renal perfusion, the *unilateral* and *marked* effect seen in the scan is not explained by this alone. Both kidneys would be affected by systemic hypotension, but the stenosis makes the stenotic kidney uniquely vulnerable to the loss of Ang II-mediated efferent constriction. The primary mechanism is the loss of efferent tone *specifically* in the stenotic kidney.
- (C) **Incorrect.** Captopril does not directly inhibit the Na+/K+-ATPase pump in the distal tubule. Its primary action is inhibiting ACE. While ACE inhibitors can affect electrolyte balance indirectly, this is not the mechanism underlying the captopril scan result.
- (E) **Incorrect.** Captopril *decreases* Ang II levels. Ang II causes afferent arteriolar constriction. Therefore, captopril would lead to afferent arteriolar *dilation*, not constriction. Furthermore, the primary effect in RAS is the loss of efferent constriction, which is critical for maintaining GFR. Afferent constriction is less important in maintaining GFR in this scenario compared to efferent constriction.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Captopril renal scintigraphy is a diagnostic test for hemodynamically significant renal artery stenosis. The test relies on the principle that the stenotic kidney maintains GFR primarily through Angiotensin II-mediated efferent arteriolar constriction. Blocking Angiotensin II synthesis with captopril causes efferent dilation, leading to a collapse in intraglomerular pressure and a marked, acute drop in GFR in the affected kidney, which is detected as reduced tracer uptake. This differentiates it from non-stenotic kidneys or kidneys where GFR is maintained primarily by other mechanisms. Key differentials include other causes of unilateral renal dysfunction (e.g., obstruction, infarction) which would not show this specific response to captopril.

<a id='question-114'></a>

### Question 114: SGLT2 Inhibitors and Renal Hemodynamics
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: SGLT2 inhibitors increase proximal NaCl delivery, activating tubuloglomerular feedback (TGF) via the macula densa, leading to afferent arteriolar constriction and a transient decrease in GFR (hemodynamic dip). (SGLT2抑制剂增加近端NaCl递送，激活通过斑块密集的肾小球滤液反馈(TGF)，导致肾小球入球动脉收缩和GFR的短暂下降（血流动力学下降）)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 56-year-old male with type 2 diabetes mellitus and established chronic kidney disease (CKD) presents for follow-up. His baseline laboratory values include a serum creatinine of 1.3 mg/dL, an estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) of 55 mL/min/1.73 m², and a urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR) of 450 mg/g. Due to his diabetes and CKD, he is initiated on empagliflozin, a selective inhibitor of the sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 (SGLT2). At his 4-week follow-up appointment, his serum creatinine has increased to 1.5 mg/dL, resulting in an eGFR decrease to 48 mL/min/1.73 m². His UACR has decreased to 225 mg/g. The patient is asymptomatic. His nephrologist explains that this initial decrease in eGFR is an expected "hemodynamic dip" related to the medication's mechanism of action. What is the primary physiological feedback mechanism responsible for this initial reduction in GFR observed after starting an SGLT2 inhibitor?

(A) Increased angiotensin II levels leading to efferent arteriolar vasoconstriction and decreased GFR.
(B) Decreased atrial natriuretic peptide (ANP) secretion leading to afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction and decreased GFR.
(C) Increased NaCl delivery to the macula densa reactivating tubuloglomerular feedback, triggering afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction.
(D) Decreased proximal tubule reabsorption of bicarbonate leading to systemic metabolic acidosis and decreased GFR.
(E) Increased glomerular capillary hydrostatic pressure due to enhanced sodium reabsorption in the distal nephron, leading to glomerular hyperfiltration injury.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 56-year-old male with T2DM and CKD (eGFR 55, UACR 450 mg/g). This establishes a baseline of impaired kidney function and albuminuria.
- **Intervention:** Started on empagliflozin (SGLT2 inhibitor). This is the key intervention whose effect is being questioned.
- **Outcome:** 4 weeks later, serum creatinine increased (1.3 -> 1.5 mg/dL), eGFR decreased (55 -> 48 mL/min/1.73 m²), and UACR decreased (450 -> 225 mg/g). This shows an initial *decrease* in GFR (the "hemodynamic dip") concurrent with a *decrease* in albuminuria (a sign of long-term renoprotection).
- **Nephrologist Explanation:** The decrease in GFR is attributed to a "hemodynamic dip" related to the drug's mechanism.
- **Question:** Asks for the *primary physiological feedback mechanism* causing the initial GFR dip.

The key clues are the drug (SGLT2 inhibitor), the initial GFR decrease despite long-term renoprotective effects (albuminuria reduction), and the nephrologist's explanation linking it to hemodynamics. This points towards a mechanism involving renal blood flow regulation.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **SGLT2 Inhibitor Action:** Empagliflozin inhibits SGLT2 in the proximal convoluted tubule (PCT), specifically the S1 and S2 segments. SGLT2 normally reabsorbs ~90% of filtered glucose and associated sodium (Na+).
- **Effect on Proximal Tubule:** By inhibiting SGLT2, empagliflozin blocks the reabsorption of filtered glucose and Na+. This leads to increased glucose excretion (glucosuria) and increased Na+ delivery downstream in the nephron.
- **Increased NaCl Delivery:** The increased Na+ load reaches the thick ascending limb (TAL) of the loop of Henle. The TAL actively reabsorbs Na+, K+, and Cl- via the NKCC2 cotransporter.
- **Macula Densa Sensing:** The increased NaCl delivery to the TAL stimulates the macula densa cells, which are specialized epithelial cells in the TAL adjacent to the afferent arteriole.
- **Tubuloglomerular Feedback (TGF):** The macula densa senses the increased NaCl concentration in the tubular fluid. This is the key step in TGF.
- **TGF Signaling Cascade:** Increased intracellular NaCl in macula densa cells leads to:
    1.  Increased activity of the Na+-K+-2Cl- cotransporter (NKCC2).
    2.  Increased ATP hydrolysis.
    3.  Release of extracellular ATP.
    4.  Conversion of ATP to adenosine by ectonucleotidases (CD39 and CD73).
    5.  Adenosine binds to A1 receptors on the smooth muscle cells of the afferent arteriole.
- **Afferent Arteriolar Vasoconstriction:** Activation of A1 receptors causes vasoconstriction of the afferent arteriole.
- **Effect on Glomerular Hemodynamics:** Afferent arteriolar constriction increases resistance to blood flow into the glomerulus. This leads to:
    1.  Decreased renal plasma flow (RPF).
    2.  Decreased glomerular capillary hydrostatic pressure (Pgc).
    3.  Decreased glomerular filtration rate (GFR).
- **"Hemodynamic Dip":** This acute, reversible decrease in GFR (typically 5-8 mL/min/1.73 m²) is known as the hemodynamic dip and occurs within the first few weeks of starting an SGLT2 inhibitor.
- **Long-Term Effects:** Despite the initial dip, the reduction in intraglomerular hypertension (due to afferent constriction) is thought to be renoprotective over the long term, slowing the progression of CKD and reducing albuminuria, as seen in the patient's case after 4 weeks and 3 years.

##### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Incorrect.** Angiotensin II primarily causes *efferent* arteriolar vasoconstriction. While this decreases GFR, it *increases* intraglomerular pressure. SGLT2 inhibitors do not primarily work by increasing angiotensin II. Furthermore, the mechanism described (efferent constriction) is opposite to the TGF pathway activated by SGLT2 inhibitors (afferent constriction).
- (B) **Incorrect.** ANP promotes vasodilation, particularly of the *afferent* arteriole, which would *increase* GFR, not decrease it. SGLT2 inhibitors do not primarily act by decreasing ANP secretion.
- (D) **Incorrect.** While SGLT2 inhibitors do affect proximal tubule function, the primary effect leading to the GFR dip is not related to bicarbonate reabsorption or systemic acidosis. Proximal bicarbonate reabsorption is largely unaffected by SGLT2 inhibition. Systemic acidosis is not a typical or primary consequence of SGLT2 inhibitors.
- (E) **Incorrect.** SGLT2 inhibitors *reduce* glomerular capillary hydrostatic pressure by causing afferent arteriolar constriction via TGF. Increased pressure due to enhanced distal sodium reabsorption would lead to hyperfiltration and glomerular injury, which is the opposite of the initial effect and the long-term goal of therapy.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
SGLT2 inhibitors cause an initial decrease in GFR ("hemodynamic dip") due to increased proximal NaCl delivery activating tubuloglomerular feedback (TGF), leading to afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction. This contrasts with other drugs like ACE inhibitors/ARBs which cause efferent arteriolar vasodilation and a decrease in intraglomerular pressure, or diuretics like loop diuretics which can cause afferent arteriolar constriction via macula densa activation but also volume depletion. The long-term effect of SGLT2 inhibitors is renoprotective, likely due to sustained reduction in intraglomerular hypertension.

---

<a id='question-115'></a>

### Question 115: Calcineurin Inhibitor Nephrotoxicity
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Calcineurin inhibitors (CNIs) like tacrolimus cause acute nephrotoxicity primarily through afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction, leading to decreased renal plasma flow (RPF) and glomerular filtration rate (GFR). (钙调神经磷酸酶抑制剂（如他克莫司）引起的急性肾毒性主要通过肾脏输入小动脉收缩介导，导致肾血流量（RPF）和滤球滤过率（GFR）下降。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 42-year-old male patient, 3 years post-renal transplantation, presents with a gradual increase in serum creatinine from a baseline of 1.1 mg/dL to 2.1 mg/dL over the past month. He also reports new-onset hypertension, with blood pressure readings consistently above 160/100 mmHg. His maintenance immunosuppression regimen includes tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. A recent tacrolimus trough level is elevated at 16 ng/mL (therapeutic range typically 5-10 ng/mL). Urinalysis shows no significant proteinuria or hematuria. Renal biopsy reveals isometric vacuolization of proximal tubular epithelial cells and prominent hyaline arteriolosclerosis, particularly affecting the afferent arterioles. The transplant nephrologist suspects calcineurin inhibitor (CNI) nephrotoxicity. What is the primary microvascular hemodynamic mechanism responsible for the acute decline in renal function observed in this patient?

(A) Efferent arteriolar vasoconstriction leading to increased intraglomerular pressure and hyperfiltration.
(B) Increased production of vasodilatory prostaglandins (PGE2, PGI2) causing afferent arteriolar dilation and decreased RPF.
(C) Intense afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction mediated by increased endothelin-1 production and decreased nitric oxide synthesis.
(D) Mesangial cell contraction leading to glomerular capillary loop compression and reduced filtration surface area.
(E) Increased permeability of the glomerular basement membrane causing protein leakage and decreased oncotic pressure, leading to edema and reduced GFR.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 42-year-old renal transplant recipient on tacrolimus (a CNI). This immediately raises suspicion for CNI-related complications.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Gradual rise in serum creatinine (from 1.1 to 2.1 mg/dL) indicates worsening renal function. New-onset hypertension is a common feature of CNI nephrotoxicity.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated tacrolimus trough level (16 ng/mL) confirms potential drug toxicity. Normal urinalysis (no significant proteinuria/hematuria) suggests the primary issue is not glomerular damage (like GN) or tubular injury (like ATN) causing significant protein/blood loss, but rather a hemodynamic issue.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Isometric vacuolization of proximal tubules is a characteristic finding of CNI toxicity, reflecting mitochondrial dysfunction and altered cellular metabolism. Hyaline arteriolosclerosis, especially of afferent arterioles, is a hallmark of chronic CNI nephrotoxicity but can also be present acutely, reflecting microvascular injury.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *primary microvascular hemodynamic mechanism* mediating the *acute* decline in renal function.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **CNI Mechanism:** Tacrolimus inhibits calcineurin, a phosphatase crucial for activating NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells). NFAT normally translocates to the nucleus and promotes transcription of genes like IL-2. By inhibiting calcineurin, tacrolimus blocks NFAT activation and suppresses T-cell proliferation, which is its desired immunosuppressive effect.
- **Acute CNI Nephrotoxicity:** This occurs relatively early after starting CNI therapy or with dose increases/toxicity. The primary mechanism involves intense, dose-dependent vasoconstriction of the *afferent arterioles*.
    - **Molecular Mediators:** CNIs stimulate the production of potent vasoconstrictors like *endothelin-1* (ET-1) and *thromboxane A2*. Simultaneously, they suppress the synthesis of vasodilators, primarily *nitric oxide* (NO) produced by endothelial nitric oxide synthase (eNOS) and *prostaglandins* (PGE2, PGI2).
    - **Hemodynamic Consequences:** The net effect is severe afferent arteriolar constriction. This constriction increases resistance to blood flow into the glomerulus.
    - **Renal Function Impact:** Reduced blood flow into the glomerulus directly decreases *renal plasma flow (RPF)*. Since GFR is largely dependent on RPF, GFR also decreases significantly. The decrease in GFR leads to the rise in serum creatinine observed in the patient. The hypertension is also a direct consequence of the systemic vasoconstriction and sodium/water retention associated with reduced RPF.
- **Chronic CNI Nephrotoxicity:** This develops over months to years. The initial acute hemodynamic changes (afferent vasoconstriction) lead to chronic ischemia of the microvasculature. This chronic ischemia triggers progressive arteriolar hyalinosis (deposition of hyaline material in arteriolar walls), interstitial fibrosis, and tubular atrophy. The biopsy findings of hyaline arteriolosclerosis and isometric vacuolization reflect this chronic process superimposed on the acute hemodynamic effects.
- **Reversibility:** Acute CNI nephrotoxicity is often reversible upon dose reduction or discontinuation of the CNI, as the vasoconstriction resolves. Chronic changes are generally irreversible.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** CNIs cause *afferent* arteriolar vasoconstriction, not efferent. Efferent arteriolar constriction would *increase* intraglomerular pressure and potentially GFR (initially), although it would also decrease RPF. This is the mechanism of action of NSAIDs, not CNIs.
- (B): **Incorrect.** CNIs *suppress* the production of vasodilatory prostaglandins (PGE2, PGI2), they do not increase it. Increased prostaglandins would cause vasodilation, increasing RPF and GFR, the opposite of what occurs in CNI nephrotoxicity.
- (D): **Incorrect.** While mesangial cell contraction can occur in certain glomerular diseases (e.g., IgA nephropathy, minimal change disease), it is not the primary mechanism of CNI nephrotoxicity. CNI toxicity primarily affects the arterioles, not the mesangium directly in this manner. Mesangial contraction reduces the filtration surface area, but the dominant effect in CNI toxicity is reduced blood flow due to arteriolar constriction.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Increased glomerular basement membrane permeability leading to protein leakage is characteristic of conditions like minimal change disease or focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS), not CNI nephrotoxicity. While CNIs can sometimes be associated with proteinuria, the primary acute mechanism is hemodynamic, not increased permeability. Furthermore, the urinalysis in the vignette showed no significant proteinuria.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Calcineurin inhibitors (tacrolimus, cyclosporine) cause acute nephrotoxicity primarily via intense afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction, mediated by increased endothelin-1 and decreased nitric oxide/prostaglandins, leading to decreased RPF and GFR. This is a dose-dependent and often reversible effect. Key differentials for acute kidney injury in transplant recipients include acute rejection, ATN (often from ischemia-reperfusion or nephrotoxic drugs other than CNIs), and CNI toxicity. Understanding the specific hemodynamic changes caused by CNIs is crucial for diagnosis and management.

<a id='question-116'></a>

### Question 116: Contrast-Induced Nephropathy (CIN)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Contrast-induced nephropathy (CIN) pathophysiology involves severe intrarenal vasoconstriction leading to medullary ischemia, particularly affecting the outer medulla due to its inherent low oxygen tension. (对比剂肾病 (CIN) 的病理生理机制涉及严重的肾内血管收缩，导致髓质缺血，特别是由于其固有的低氧张力而影响外髓质。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 72-year-old male with a history of type 2 diabetes mellitus and chronic kidney disease (baseline eGFR 35 mL/min/1.73m²) presents to the emergency department after undergoing coronary angiography. During the procedure, 150 mL of iso-osmolar iodinated radiocontrast was administered. Forty-eight hours post-procedure, the patient develops oliguria (urine output < 400 mL/day). Laboratory tests reveal a rise in serum creatinine from 1.8 mg/dL to 3.2 mg/dL. Urinalysis shows muddy brown casts. Renal ultrasound is unremarkable. The interventional cardiologist explains that contrast-induced acute kidney injury (AKI) is a common complication, particularly in patients with pre-existing renal dysfunction, and involves a complex interplay of hemodynamic and direct tubular effects. The most critical factor contributing to the acute decline in renal function in this patient is the intense vasoconstriction induced by the contrast agent.

Which specific region of the kidney is most vulnerable to ischemic damage secondary to this intense vasoconstriction?

(A) The renal cortex, primarily affecting the glomeruli and proximal convoluted tubules due to their high metabolic demand.
(B) The renal inner medulla, which is relatively resistant to ischemia due to its countercurrent exchange system.
(C) The renal outer medulla, containing the thick ascending limb of the loop of Henle and proximal S3 segment of the collecting duct, due to its inherently low oxygen tension.
(D) The juxtamedullary nephrons, which are less susceptible to ischemic injury compared to cortical nephrons.
(E) The vasa recta, which are primarily affected by systemic rather than intrarenal vasoconstriction.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 72-year-old male with diabetes and pre-existing CKD (eGFR 35) - High-risk factors for CIN.
- **Procedure:** Coronary angiography with 150 mL iodinated contrast - Trigger for CIN.
- **Timing:** AKI develops 48 hours post-contrast - Typical timeframe for CIN.
- **Clinical Findings:** Oliguria, rise in serum creatinine (1.8 to 3.2 mg/dL) - Diagnostic criteria for AKI.
- **Urinalysis:** Muddy brown casts - Suggestive of acute tubular necrosis (ATN), a common manifestation of CIN.
- **Pathophysiology Hint:** Cardiologist mentions intense vasoconstriction as a key factor.
- **Question:** Asks for the *most vulnerable* renal region to ischemia from this vasoconstriction.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Contrast-Induced Nephropathy (CIN):** A form of acute kidney injury (AKI) occurring after exposure to iodinated radiocontrast media.
- **Pathophysiology:** CIN involves two main mechanisms:
    1.  **Hemodynamic Effects:** Iodinated contrast causes a transient, intense intrarenal vasoconstriction. This is mediated by several factors, including:
        *   **Endothelin-1 (ET-1):** Released by tubular cells and endothelial cells in response to contrast. ET-1 is a potent vasoconstrictor.
        *   **Reactive Oxygen Species (ROS):** Generated by contrast, leading to endothelial dysfunction and vasoconstriction.
        *   **Loss of Vasodilators:** Contrast may inhibit the production or action of vasodilators like nitric oxide (NO) and prostacyclin.
        *   **Adenosine:** Also implicated in the vasoconstrictive response.
    2.  **Direct Tubular Toxicity:** High osmolality and viscosity of contrast can cause direct damage to tubular cells, particularly in the proximal tubule and thick ascending limb (TAL), leading to ATN.
- **Regional Vulnerability to Ischemia:** The renal medulla, particularly the outer medulla, is inherently more susceptible to ischemia than the cortex. This is because:
    *   **Low Baseline Oxygen Tension (PO2):** The renal cortex has a PO2 of ~60 mmHg, while the outer medulla has a PO2 of only ~15 mmHg. This low PO2 is due to the long, tortuous path of blood flow through the vasa recta and the high metabolic activity of the medullary tubules (TAL and collecting ducts).
    *   **High Metabolic Demand:** The TAL and S3 segment of the collecting duct have high metabolic activity (e.g., Na+-K+-2Cl- cotransport in TAL, urea transport and water reabsorption in collecting duct), requiring significant oxygen consumption.
    *   **Vasoconstriction:** The intense vasoconstriction induced by contrast further reduces blood flow (RPF) to the medulla, exacerbating the existing hypoxic state.
- **Conclusion:** The combination of inherently low baseline PO2 and high metabolic demand makes the renal outer medulla, containing the TAL and S3 segment, the most vulnerable region to ischemic injury during CIN. The muddy brown casts seen in the urinalysis are consistent with ATN, which often originates in the proximal tubule and TAL.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **The renal cortex, primarily affecting the glomeruli and proximal convoluted tubules due to their high metabolic demand.** *Incorrect.* While the cortex has high metabolic demand, its baseline PO2 is much higher (~60 mmHg) than the medulla. The primary ischemic damage in CIN occurs in the medulla due to its pre-existing hypoxia, which is worsened by vasoconstriction. Glomeruli are also affected by reduced GFR, but the *most severe ischemic necrosis* occurs medullarly.
- (B): **The renal inner medulla, which is relatively resistant to ischemia due to its countercurrent exchange system.** *Incorrect.* The inner medulla has an even lower PO2 (~10 mmHg) than the outer medulla. However, the countercurrent exchange system (vasa recta) is crucial for maintaining this low PO2 and preventing further oxygen depletion, but it doesn't make the inner medulla *resistant* to ischemia. In fact, it's also highly vulnerable. The question asks for the *most* vulnerable region, and the outer medulla, containing the TAL and S3 segment, is typically considered the primary site of ischemic damage in CIN due to the combined factors of low PO2 and high metabolic activity in these specific segments. Furthermore, the TAL is directly exposed to the contrast agent, contributing to direct toxicity alongside ischemia.
- (D): **The juxtamedullary nephrons, which are less susceptible to ischemic injury compared to cortical nephrons.** *Incorrect.* Juxtamedullary nephrons have long loops of Henle extending deep into the medulla. Their TAL and distal convoluted tubules are located in the outer medulla, making them *more* susceptible to medullary ischemia than cortical nephrons, which primarily reside in the cortex.
- (E): **The vasa recta, which are primarily affected by systemic rather than intrarenal vasoconstriction.** *Incorrect.* While systemic vasoconstriction can occur, the primary mechanism of CIN involves *intrarenal* vasoconstriction mediated by contrast agents. The vasa recta are the blood vessels supplying the medulla, and their constriction is central to the development of medullary ischemia in CIN.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Contrast-induced nephropathy (CIN) is characterized by intense intrarenal vasoconstriction, leading to medullary ischemia. The renal outer medulla, containing the thick ascending limb (TAL) and S3 segment of the collecting duct, is the most vulnerable region due to its inherently low baseline oxygen tension (~15 mmHg) and high metabolic activity. This contrasts with the cortex (~60 mmHg PO2) and inner medulla (~10 mmHg PO2). Key differentials for AKI include prerenal azotemia (FeNa < 1%, FeUrea < 35%, UOsm > 500 mOsm/kg), ATN (FeNa > 2%, FeUrea > 35%, UOsm < 350 mOsm/kg, muddy brown casts), and glomerulonephritis (proteinuria, hematuria, RBC casts). CIN typically presents as ATN.

<a id='question-117'></a>

### Question 117: Aminoglycoside Nephrotoxicity: Proximal Tubule Uptake Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Aminoglycoside nephrotoxicity involves uptake by proximal tubule cells via receptor-mediated endocytosis, specifically the megalin-cubilin complex, leading to lysosomal accumulation and cell injury. (氨基糖苷肾毒性涉及近曲小管细胞通过受体介导的内吞作用吸收，特别是巨球蛋白-卵圆形蛋白复合物，导致溶酶体积累和细胞损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 60-year-old female with a history of hypertension and type 2 diabetes mellitus is hospitalized for community-acquired Pseudomonas aeruginosa urosepsis. She is treated with intravenous gentamicin (7 mg/kg/day) for 8 days. On day 8, she develops non-oliguric acute kidney injury (AKI). Her serum creatinine increases from a baseline of 0.9 mg/dL to 2.8 mg/dL. Urinalysis reveals 2+ proteinuria, glucosuria despite normal serum glucose levels, and muddy brown granular casts. Renal biopsy shows proximal tubular epithelial cell injury with cytoplasmic vacuoles containing myeloid bodies. Fractional excretion of sodium (FeNa) is 1.5%.

Which of the following receptor complexes on the apical membrane of proximal tubule cells is primarily responsible for the initial uptake of gentamicin?

(A) Low-density lipoprotein receptor-related protein 1 (LRP1)
(B) Megalin-cubilin receptor complex
(C) Sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 (SGLT2)
(D) Aquaporin-1 (AQP1)
(E) Organic anion transporter 1 (OAT1)

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 60-year-old female with comorbidities (HTN, DM2) treated with IV gentamicin for Pseudomonas urosepsis. This sets the stage for potential drug-induced AKI, specifically aminoglycoside nephrotoxicity.
- **Timing:** AKI develops on Day 8 of therapy. Aminoglycoside nephrotoxicity typically manifests after 5-10 days of sustained exposure.
- **AKI Characteristics:** Non-oliguric AKI (rise in creatinine from 0.9 to 2.8 mg/dL). This is characteristic of aminoglycoside toxicity, which primarily affects the tubules rather than causing glomerular damage or obstruction leading to oliguria.
- **Urinalysis Findings:**
    - Proteinuria (2+): Indicates tubular damage, as aminoglycosides primarily injure proximal tubule cells.
    - Glucosuria with normal blood sugar: Classic sign of proximal tubule dysfunction (Fanconi syndrome), as aminoglycosides impair glucose reabsorption via SGLT2.
    - Muddy brown granular casts: Suggests tubular cell sloughing and degeneration, consistent with acute tubular necrosis (ATN), often seen in aminoglycoside toxicity.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Proximal tubular epithelial cell injury with cytoplasmic vacuoles containing myeloid bodies. Myeloid bodies are pathognomonic for aminoglycoside toxicity, representing accumulations of undigested lysosomal material (phospholipids) within lysosomes.
- **FeNa:** 1.5%. This value is relatively low, but aminoglycoside nephrotoxicity is often considered a pre-renal or intrinsic AKI with variable FeNa, sometimes <1% if volume depletion coexists or >1% if tubular damage is significant. The key is the constellation of findings pointing to proximal tubule injury.
- **Question Focus:** The question asks about the specific receptor complex mediating gentamicin uptake in the proximal tubule.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Aminoglycoside Properties:** Aminoglycosides (e.g., gentamicin, tobramycin, amikacin) are polycationic molecules.
- **Glomerular Filtration:** They are freely filtered by the glomerulus due to their small size and charge.
- **Tubular Reabsorption:** The primary mechanism of toxicity involves reabsorption by proximal tubule cells. This reabsorption is not passive; it is an active, receptor-mediated endocytic process.
- **Receptor Identification:** The key receptor complex responsible for binding polycationic molecules like aminoglycosides on the apical membrane of proximal tubule cells is the megalin-cubilin complex.
    - **Megalin (LRP2):** A large, multi-ligand endocytic receptor (a member of the low-density lipoprotein receptor family). It is highly expressed on the apical surface of proximal tubule cells. Its anionic nature allows it to bind positively charged molecules.
    - **Cubilin (CUBN):** A transmembrane protein that forms a complex with megalin. It is essential for the efficient endocytosis of ligands bound by megalin.
- **Endocytosis Pathway:** Binding of gentamicin to the megalin-cubilin complex triggers clathrin-mediated endocytosis, internalizing the drug into early endosomes.
- **Lysosomal Trafficking:** The endosomes mature into lysosomes. Aminoglycosides accumulate within the lysosomes.
- **Mechanism of Toxicity:** Inside the lysosomes, aminoglycosides inhibit lysosomal phospholipases and interfere with lysosomal membrane stability. This leads to the formation of large, undigested lipid aggregates called myeloid bodies (or lamellar bodies). The accumulation of myeloid bodies disrupts lysosomal function, leading to lysosomal rupture and release of toxic contents (proteases, aminoglycosides) into the cytoplasm. This triggers mitochondrial dysfunction, oxidative stress, and ultimately apoptotic or necrotic cell death of the proximal tubule epithelial cells.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Low-density lipoprotein receptor-related protein 1 (LRP1):** LRP1 is another member of the LDL receptor family involved in endocytosis, primarily known for its role in clearing lipoproteins, proteases, and other ligands. While it is expressed in the kidney, it is not the primary receptor for aminoglycoside uptake in proximal tubules. Megalin (LRP2) is the key receptor here.
- (C) **Sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 (SGLT2):** SGLT2 is located on the apical membrane of proximal tubule cells and is responsible for the reabsorption of glucose from the tubular fluid. Aminoglycosides inhibit SGLT2, leading to glucosuria (as seen in the vignette), but SGLT2 itself does not mediate the uptake of aminoglycosides.
- (D) **Aquaporin-1 (AQP1):** AQP1 is a water channel protein expressed on both the apical and basolateral membranes of proximal tubule cells, facilitating water reabsorption. It is not involved in the transport or uptake of aminoglycosides.
- (E) **Organic anion transporter 1 (OAT1):** OAT1 is located on the basolateral membrane of proximal tubule cells and is responsible for the secretion of organic anions (like urate, salicylate, penicillin) into the blood. Aminoglycosides are polycations, not organic anions, and their primary uptake occurs via the apical membrane receptor complex.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Aminoglycoside nephrotoxicity is a classic example of drug-induced AKI mediated by receptor-mediated endocytosis in the proximal tubule. The key receptor complex is megalin-cubilin, which binds the polycationic drug, leading to lysosomal accumulation and cell injury. Remember the characteristic features: non-oliguric AKI, Fanconi syndrome (glucosuria, proteinuria, phosphaturia), and myeloid bodies on biopsy. Differentiate this mechanism from other causes of ATN (e.g., ischemia, contrast dye) or glomerular diseases.

<a id='question-118'></a>

### Question 118: Amphotericin B Nephrotoxicity
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Amphotericin B causes dose-dependent renal vasoconstriction (afferent arterioles) and distal tubular damage (pore formation), leading to distal renal tubular acidosis (Type 1 dRTA), severe hypokalemia, and hypomagnesemia. (两性霉素B导致剂量依赖性肾血管收缩（肾小动脉）和远端小管损伤（孔形成），导致远端肾小管酸中毒（1型dRTA）、严重低钾血症和低镁血症。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male patient with acute myeloid leukemia undergoing induction chemotherapy develops invasive pulmonary aspergillosis. He is treated with intravenous amphotericin B deoxycholate. Over the next 10 days, his serum creatinine increases from 0.8 mg/dL to 2.5 mg/dL, and he develops significant volume overload. Laboratory studies reveal serum potassium of 2.8 mEq/L (normal 3.5-5.0 mEq/L), serum magnesium of 1.1 mg/dL (normal 1.7-2.2 mg/dL), and a non-anion gap metabolic acidosis with a serum bicarbonate level of 12 mEq/L (normal 22-28 mEq/L). Arterial blood gas shows pH 7.30 (normal 7.35-7.45) and pCO2 of 30 mmHg (normal 35-45 mmHg). Urinalysis reveals a urine pH of 6.0. Renal ultrasound shows normal kidney size and echotexture. Which of the following electrolyte abnormalities and tubular transport defects most accurately describes the pathophysiology of this patient's acute kidney injury?

(A) Proximal renal tubular acidosis (Type 2 dRTA) characterized by bicarbonate wasting and hypophosphatemia.
(B) Loop diuretic-induced nephropathy characterized by hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia, and metabolic alkalosis.
(C) Distal renal tubular acidosis (Type 1 dRTA) characterized by impaired distal hydrogen ion secretion, leading to hypokalemia and metabolic acidosis with inappropriately alkaline urine.
(D) Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) characterized by hyponatremia, concentrated urine, and euvolemia.
(E) Acute tubular necrosis (ATN) characterized by muddy brown casts, fractional excretion of sodium (FeNa) > 2%, and impaired reabsorption of all solutes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male with leukemia undergoing chemotherapy, high risk for fungal infections.
- **Medication:** Intravenous amphotericin B deoxycholate. This is a key clue, as amphotericin B is known for its nephrotoxicity.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Progressive acute kidney injury (Cr 0.8 -> 2.5 mg/dL), volume overload. This indicates significant renal dysfunction.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - **Hypokalemia (K+ 2.8 mEq/L):** Significant potassium loss.
    - **Hypomagnesemia (Mg2+ 1.1 mg/dL):** Significant magnesium loss.
    - **Non-anion gap metabolic acidosis (HCO3- 12 mEq/L, pH 7.30, pCO2 30 mmHg):** Indicates a primary metabolic acidosis, likely due to bicarbonate loss or impaired acid excretion. The respiratory compensation (low pCO2) is appropriate.
    - **Urine pH 6.0:** This is the most critical clue. In the setting of systemic metabolic acidosis (serum HCO3- 12 mEq/L), the kidneys should normally acidify the urine (pH < 5.5) to excrete excess acid. A urine pH > 5.5 despite systemic acidosis indicates impaired distal acidification.
- **Renal Ultrasound:** Normal, ruling out obstructive uropathy.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific tubular transport defect and electrolyte abnormalities characteristic of amphotericin B nephrotoxicity.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Amphotericin B Mechanism:** Amphotericin B is an antifungal agent that binds to ergosterol in fungal cell membranes, forming pores that increase membrane permeability and cause leakage of intracellular contents, leading to cell death.
- **Renal Toxicity Mechanism:** Amphotericin B also binds to cholesterol in mammalian cell membranes, particularly in the renal tubules.
    - **Afferent Arteriolar Vasoconstriction:** Amphotericin B causes intense, dose-dependent constriction of the afferent arterioles. This reduces renal plasma flow (RPF) and glomerular filtration rate (GFR), leading to pre-renal azotemia (increased BUN and creatinine). This explains the AKI and volume overload.
    - **Distal Tubular Damage:** Amphotericin B forms pores in the membranes of distal tubular cells (principal cells and intercalated cells). These pores disrupt ion transport.
        - **Potassium Wasting:** The pores increase membrane permeability, leading to passive leakage of potassium ions from the tubular cells into the tubular lumen, causing severe hypokalemia.
        - **Magnesium Wasting:** Similar pore formation leads to increased magnesium loss in the urine, causing severe hypomagnesemia.
        - **Impaired Hydrogen Ion Secretion:** The pores disrupt the function of H+-ATPases in the apical membrane of alpha-intercalated cells, impairing the secretion of H+ into the tubular lumen. This leads to distal renal tubular acidosis (Type 1 dRTA).
- **Distal RTA (Type 1):** Characterized by the inability of the distal tubule to secrete H+ effectively. This results in:
    - **Metabolic Acidosis:** Failure to excrete acid load leads to systemic metabolic acidosis.
    - **Inappropriately Alkaline Urine:** Despite systemic acidosis, the urine pH remains > 5.5 because H+ cannot be secreted into the urine.
    - **Hypokalemia:** Impaired H+ secretion is often coupled with K+ secretion via H+/K+-ATPase, leading to increased K+ loss. Also, the increased distal tubular flow due to osmotic diuresis from wasting solutes can enhance K+ secretion.
    - **Nephrocalcinosis/Nephrolithiasis:** Chronic acidosis can lead to calcium phosphate stone formation.
- **Patient's Findings:** The patient exhibits AKI (likely due to afferent vasoconstriction), severe hypokalemia, severe hypomagnesemia, non-anion gap metabolic acidosis, and a urine pH > 5.5 despite systemic acidosis. This constellation of findings is classic for amphotericin B-induced distal RTA.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Proximal renal tubular acidosis (Type 2 dRTA):** This involves impaired bicarbonate reabsorption in the proximal tubule. It causes metabolic acidosis, but the urine pH is typically < 5.5 (the proximal tubule can still acidify the urine). It is also associated with phosphaturia and glycosuria (Fanconi syndrome), which are not mentioned here. Amphotericin B primarily affects distal tubules, not proximal tubules.
- (B) **Loop diuretic-induced nephropathy:** Loop diuretics (e.g., furosemide) inhibit the Na+-K+-2Cl- cotransporter in the thick ascending limb. This causes volume contraction, metabolic alkalosis (due to H+ loss and volume contraction stimulating aldosterone), hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia, and hypocalcemia. The urine pH is typically alkaline (> 5.5) due to increased distal delivery of bicarbonate and H+ secretion stimulated by volume contraction and hypokalemia. However, the primary presentation here is metabolic *acidosis*, not alkalosis, and the underlying cause is amphotericin B, not a loop diuretic.
- (D) **Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH):** This involves excessive ADH release, leading to water retention, hyponatremia, concentrated urine (high urine osmolarity), and euvolemia or mild hypervolemia. It does not cause metabolic acidosis, hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia, or AKI in this manner.
- (E) **Acute tubular necrosis (ATN):** This is characterized by damage to tubular epithelial cells, often resulting from ischemia or toxins. It typically presents with AKI, FeNa > 2%, muddy brown casts in the urine, and impaired reabsorption of various solutes. While amphotericin B can cause AKI, the specific electrolyte abnormalities (hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia) and the characteristic finding of distal RTA (urine pH > 5.5 with acidosis) are not typical features of ATN itself, although ATN can coexist. The primary mechanism described in the vignette points specifically to distal tubular dysfunction.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Amphotericin B nephrotoxicity is characterized by dual mechanisms: afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction causing pre-renal AKI and distal tubular pore formation leading to Type 1 distal RTA, severe hypokalemia, and hypomagnesemia. Remember the key finding of metabolic acidosis with inappropriately alkaline urine (pH > 5.5). Differentiate this from other causes of AKI and electrolyte disturbances like ATN, loop diuretics, or other forms of RTA.

---

<a id='question-119'></a>

### Question 119: Cisplatin Nephrotoxicity and Proximal Tubule Transport
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Cisplatin is a chemotherapeutic agent known for its nephrotoxicity, primarily affecting the proximal tubules. Its uptake into these cells is mediated by a specific basolateral membrane transporter. (顺铂的肾毒性主要影响近曲小管，其进入这些细胞的机制是由特定的基底外膜转运蛋白介导的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with metastatic non-small cell lung cancer is undergoing his third cycle of cisplatin-based chemotherapy. Five days after receiving the infusion, he presents to the emergency department with fatigue and decreased urine output. His serum creatinine has increased from a baseline of 1.0 mg/dL to 3.4 mg/dL. Laboratory tests reveal severe hypomagnesemia (serum Mg2+ = 0.8 mg/dL, normal 1.7-2.2 mg/dL), hypokalemia (serum K+ = 2.8 mEq/L, normal 3.5-5.0 mEq/L), hypophosphatemia (serum PO43- = 1.5 mg/dL, normal 2.5-4.5 mg/dL), and glucosuria despite normal serum glucose. Urinalysis shows proteinuria (3+), phosphaturia, and magnesiumuria. The patient's oncologist suspects cisplatin-induced acute kidney injury (AKI) and reviews the mechanism of cisplatin uptake by renal tubular cells. Which of the following transporters is primarily responsible for the basolateral uptake of cisplatin into proximal tubular epithelial cells, leading to its nephrotoxic effects?

(A) Multidrug resistance protein 2 (MRP2 / ABCC2)
(B) Multidrug resistance-associated protein 1 (MRP1 / ABCC1)
(C) Organic anion transporter 1 (OAT1 / SLC22A6)
(D) Organic cation transporter 2 (OCT2 / SLC22A2)
(E) Sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 (SGLT2 / SLC5A2)

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male with metastatic non-small cell lung cancer receiving cisplatin chemotherapy. This immediately points towards potential chemotherapy side effects.
- **Timing:** Symptoms develop 5 days post-infusion, typical for cisplatin nephrotoxicity.
- **Renal Function:** Serum creatinine increase from 1.0 to 3.4 mg/dL indicates significant AKI.
- **Electrolyte Abnormalities:** Severe hypomagnesemia, hypokalemia, and hypophosphatemia are classic signs of proximal tubular dysfunction (Fanconi syndrome).
- **Urinalysis Findings:** Proteinuria, phosphaturia, and magnesiumuria further confirm proximal tubule damage and impaired reabsorption. Glucosuria despite normal serum glucose is also characteristic of proximal tubule dysfunction (SGLT2 impairment).
- **Question Focus:** The question asks about the specific basolateral membrane transporter responsible for cisplatin uptake into proximal tubular cells. This directs the focus to the cellular mechanism of cisplatin nephrotoxicity.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Cisplatin Mechanism:** Cisplatin is a platinum-based chemotherapeutic agent. It enters cells, including renal proximal tubule cells, via specific transporters.
- **Basolateral Uptake:** The primary transporter responsible for the basolateral uptake of cisplatin into proximal tubule epithelial cells is the Organic Cation Transporter 2 (OCT2), encoded by the *SLC22A2* gene. OCT2 is highly expressed in the basolateral membrane of proximal tubule cells and is responsible for the uptake of various organic cations, including metformin, choline, and certain drugs like cisplatin.
- **Intracellular Activation:** Once inside the cell, cisplatin undergoes aquation (hydration) in the relatively low chloride concentration environment of the cytoplasm. This aquated form is highly reactive and binds to DNA, forming intra- and inter-strand cross-links, particularly in the nucleus and mitochondria.
- **Mitochondrial Damage:** Cisplatin also accumulates in mitochondria, where it inhibits the electron transport chain (complexes I and IV) and generates reactive oxygen species (ROS), leading to mitochondrial dysfunction and apoptosis.
- **Proximal Tubule Specificity:** The high expression of OCT2 in the proximal tubule makes these cells particularly susceptible to cisplatin accumulation and toxicity. The S3 segment of the proximal tubule is most vulnerable.
- **Fanconi Syndrome:** The resulting damage to the proximal tubule impairs its reabsorptive functions, leading to the loss of electrolytes (Mg2+, K+, PO43-), glucose, amino acids, bicarbonate, and proteins in the urine, characteristic of Fanconi syndrome.
- **Other Transporters:**
    - MRP2 (ABCC2) is an ATP-binding cassette transporter located on the apical membrane, primarily involved in the efflux of organic anions and glutathione conjugates. It plays a role in protecting cells from cisplatin toxicity by exporting it back into the tubular lumen, but it is not the primary uptake transporter.
    - MRP1 (ABCC1) is also an ABC transporter, involved in efflux, but less significant for cisplatin uptake in the kidney compared to OCT2.
    - OAT1 (SLC22A6) is an organic anion transporter, primarily involved in the uptake of organic anions like indoxyl sulfate and p-aminohippurate. It does not transport cisplatin.
    - SGLT2 (SLC5A2) is a sodium-glucose cotransporter located on the apical membrane, responsible for glucose reabsorption. Its dysfunction contributes to glucosuria in Fanconi syndrome but is not involved in cisplatin uptake.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Multidrug resistance protein 2 (MRP2 / ABCC2): This is an apical efflux transporter. While it can export cisplatin from the cell back into the lumen, potentially mitigating toxicity, it is not the primary transporter responsible for *uptake* into the cell from the blood. Students might confuse efflux and uptake mechanisms.
- (B) Multidrug resistance-associated protein 1 (MRP1 / ABCC1): Similar to MRP2, MRP1 is an efflux transporter (ABC family). It is involved in multidrug resistance but is not the main transporter for cisplatin uptake into proximal tubule cells. Students might confuse ABC transporters with OCTs.
- (C) Organic anion transporter 1 (OAT1 / SLC22A6): This transporter is located on the basolateral membrane of proximal tubule cells but is responsible for the uptake of organic *anions*, not organic *cations* like cisplatin. Students might confuse organic cation transporters (OCTs) with organic anion transporters (OATs) based on location or function.
- (E) Sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 (SGLT2 / SLC5A2): This is an apical membrane transporter responsible for glucose reabsorption. Its dysfunction leads to glucosuria in Fanconi syndrome, but it does not transport cisplatin. Students might associate SGLT2 with proximal tubule function but not its specific role in drug transport.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cisplatin nephrotoxicity is primarily mediated by its uptake into proximal tubule cells via the basolateral Organic Cation Transporter 2 (OCT2). Understanding this specific transport mechanism is crucial for comprehending the drug's selective toxicity. Key differentials include other causes of AKI and Fanconi syndrome, but the specific transporter mechanism is unique to cisplatin's action in this context.

---

<a id='question-120'></a>

### Question 120: Tenofovir-Induced Proximal Tubular Dysfunction
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Tenofovir (TDF) is an NRTI used for HIV treatment. Its intracellular accumulation in proximal tubule cells due to impaired apical efflux via MRP4 leads to mitochondrial toxicity (inhibition of DNA polymerase-gamma), resulting in impaired ATP production and subsequent dysfunction of multiple proximal tubular transport mechanisms, causing Fanconi syndrome. (替诺福韦 (TDF) 是一种用于治疗艾滋病毒的核苷类逆转录酶抑制剂。由于 MRP4 介导的顶膜排出的受损，它在近端小管细胞内积累，导致线粒体毒性（抑制 DNA 聚合酶-γ），从而导致 ATP 产生受损，并随后导致多个近端小管转运机制功能障碍，从而引起范可尼综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 48-year-old male with a history of HIV infection has been on a stable antiretroviral therapy regimen including Tenofovir Disoproxil Fumarate (TDF) for the past 3 years. He presents to his primary care physician complaining of worsening muscle weakness and diffuse bone pain over the last 6 months. His vital signs are stable. Physical examination reveals mild muscle atrophy and tenderness over the costovertebral angles. Laboratory investigations show a serum creatinine of 2.2 mg/dL (baseline 0.8 mg/dL), serum phosphate of 1.4 mg/dL (normal range 2.5-4.5 mg/dL), serum potassium of 3.2 mEq/L (normal range 3.5-5.0 mEq/L), and a normal serum glucose of 90 mg/dL. Urinalysis is positive for glucose, protein (trace), and generalized amino acids. A 24-hour urine collection reveals significant glucosuria, phosphaturia, and aminoaciduria. Arterial blood gas analysis shows a normal anion gap metabolic acidosis (pH 7.30, HCO3- 16 mEq/L). Renal biopsy is not performed. Based on this clinical presentation and laboratory findings, which specific syndrome is most likely caused by the patient's long-term TDF use?

(A) Acute Tubular Necrosis (ATN)
(B) Minimal Change Disease
(C) Glomerulonephritis
(D) Fanconi Syndrome
(E) Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 48-year-old male with HIV on TDF for 3 years. This immediately raises suspicion for drug-induced nephrotoxicity, specifically related to TDF.
- **Symptoms:** Muscle weakness and bone pain suggest electrolyte abnormalities (hypophosphatemia, hypokalemia) and potential metabolic bone disease (osteomalacia).
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Elevated serum creatinine (2.2 mg/dL) indicates renal impairment.
    - Low serum phosphate (1.4 mg/dL) and hypokalemia (3.2 mEq/L) point towards renal tubular wasting of these electrolytes.
    - Normal serum glucose (90 mg/dL) with glucosuria is a hallmark of proximal tubular dysfunction.
    - Generalized aminoaciduria also indicates impaired proximal tubular reabsorption.
    - Metabolic acidosis (pH 7.30, HCO3- 16 mEq/L) with a normal anion gap suggests impaired bicarbonate reabsorption, typically in the proximal or distal tubule.
- **Diagnosis:** The constellation of glucosuria (with normal glycemia), phosphaturia, aminoaciduria, hypokalemia, hypophosphatemia, and metabolic acidosis points strongly towards a generalized defect in proximal tubular reabsorption and secretion.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **TDF Mechanism:** Tenofovir Disoproxil Fumarate (TDF) is a prodrug converted to tenofovir diphosphate, an NRTI used to treat HIV.
- **Renal Handling:** TDF is filtered by the glomerulus and secreted into the proximal tubule cells via organic anion transporters (OATs) located on the basolateral membrane.
- **Apical Efflux:** Normally, tenofovir diphosphate is transported out of the proximal tubule cell back into the tubular lumen via Multidrug Resistance Protein 4 (MRP4) located on the apical membrane.
- **Toxicity:** If MRP4 is saturated or its function is impaired, tenofovir diphosphate accumulates intracellularly. Tenofovir diphosphate inhibits mitochondrial DNA polymerase-gamma, leading to mitochondrial dysfunction and depletion of mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA).
- **Proximal Tubule Dependence:** Proximal tubule cells have high metabolic activity and rely heavily on mitochondrial ATP production for active transport processes.
- **Fanconi Syndrome Pathophysiology:** The resulting ATP depletion impairs the function of various transporters located on the apical and basolateral membranes of the proximal tubule cells. This leads to:
    - Failure to reabsorb glucose (glucosuria).
    - Failure to reabsorb phosphate (phosphaturia, leading to hypophosphatemia and bone disease).
    - Failure to reabsorb amino acids (aminoaciduria).
    - Failure to reabsorb bicarbonate (leading to metabolic acidosis, often Type 2 proximal RTA).
    - Failure to reabsorb potassium (leading to hypokalemia).
    - Failure to reabsorb uric acid (leading to hyperuricosuria).
    - Failure to secrete organic acids and bases.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The impaired transport functions manifest clinically as the signs and symptoms observed in the patient (muscle weakness, bone pain, electrolyte abnormalities, renal impairment, glucosuria). This constellation of findings defines Fanconi syndrome, a generalized proximal tubular disorder.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Acute Tubular Necrosis (ATN):** ATN typically presents with acute kidney injury (AKI), often following ischemic or nephrotoxic insults. Urinalysis usually shows muddy brown casts, granular casts, and renal tubular epithelial cells. While TDF can cause renal dysfunction, it typically manifests as chronic proximal tubular injury (Fanconi syndrome) rather than acute tubular necrosis. ATN does not specifically cause glucosuria with normal blood glucose, generalized aminoaciduria, and phosphaturia as the primary features.
- (B) **Minimal Change Disease:** This is a common cause of nephrotic syndrome in children, characterized by heavy proteinuria (>3.5 g/day), hypoalbuminemia, edema, and hyperlipidemia. It involves podocyte foot process effacement seen on electron microscopy but typically normal light microscopy and immunofluorescence. The patient's presentation (mild proteinuria, glucosuria, phosphaturia, aminoaciduria, metabolic acidosis) is inconsistent with nephrotic syndrome or Minimal Change Disease.
- (C) **Glomerulonephritis:** This involves inflammation of the glomeruli, typically presenting with hematuria, proteinuria (often nephrotic range depending on the type), hypertension, edema, and renal insufficiency. Urinalysis often shows red blood cell casts. The patient's presentation lacks significant hematuria or nephrotic-range proteinuria and features characteristic of proximal tubular dysfunction, making glomerulonephritis unlikely.
- (E) **Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus:** While Type 1 DM can cause glucosuria due to hyperglycemia exceeding the renal threshold for glucose reabsorption, the patient's blood glucose is normal (90 mg/dL). Furthermore, Type 1 DM does not typically cause generalized aminoaciduria, phosphaturia, hypophosphatemia, hypokalemia, or metabolic acidosis as primary features, although diabetic nephropathy can eventually lead to tubular dysfunction. The specific combination of findings in this patient points away from diabetes as the primary cause.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Tenofovir (TDF) is a common cause of drug-induced Fanconi syndrome due to mitochondrial toxicity in proximal tubule cells.
- Fanconi syndrome is characterized by generalized proximal tubular dysfunction, leading to glucosuria (with normal blood glucose), aminoaciduria, phosphaturia, hypokalemia, and metabolic acidosis (often Type 2 proximal RTA).
- Differentiating Fanconi syndrome from other causes of renal tubular dysfunction (e.g., specific transport defects like Dent's disease or Lowe syndrome) requires a full clinical picture, but the combination of findings presented here is classic for TDF-induced Fanconi syndrome.
